\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{i}\underline{\Upsilon}_{\alpha}\theta^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{i}\underline{\Upsilon}_{\bar{\alpha}}\theta^{\bar{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\Upsilon}_{\alpha}\underline{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}\underline{\theta}^{0}\,,

and
\widehat{g}=\mathrm{e}^{\underline{\varphi}}g
.

In addition,
\varpi^{*}\underline{\theta}{}^{0}=g(k,\cdot)
,
\varpi^{*}\widehat{\underline{\theta}}{}^{0}=\widehat{g}(k,\cdot)
,
\lambda=g(\ell,\cdot)
, and
\widehat{\lambda}=\widehat{g}(\widehat{\ell},\cdot)
, where
(k,\ell)
and
(\widehat{k},\widehat{\ell})
are related by (
5.2
).

Remark 5.9
.

It is shown in
[
106
]
how the Levi-Civita connection of a metric in
\accentset{n.e.}{\mathbf{c}}
relates to the Webster–Tanaka connection of its corresponding almost pseudo-Hermitian structure. In this way, one can identify
\underline{\Upsilon}_{\alpha}
in Proposition
5.8
as the
(1,0)
-part of the difference between two Webster–Tanaka connections.

Example 5.10

(Fefferman construction)
.

There is a well-known canonical construction, originally due to Fefferman
[
22
,
23
]
and later characterised by Sparling, Graham
[
29
]
(see also
[
12
,
11
]
), which associates to any contact CR structure
(\underline{\mathcal{M}},\underline{H},\underline{J})
of dimension
2m+1
a conformal structure
\mathbf{c}
of Lorentzian signature and dimension
2m+2
on the total space of a circle bundle
\mathcal{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varpi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underline{\mathcal{M}}
, namely the quotient of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{m+1}(\mathrm{Ann}(\underline{H}^{(1,0)}))
with the zero section removed, by a
\mathbf{R}_{>0}
-action. More explicitly, let
\phi
be a fiber coordinate on
\mathcal{M}
. Choose of contact form
\underline{\theta}^{0}
of
(\underline{H},\underline{J})
with Levi form
\mathsf{h}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}
and corresponding Webster–Tanaka connection
1
-form
\underline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}
,
Webster–Schouten scalar
\underline{\mathsf{P}}
, and define the
1
-form

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\phi+\frac{1}{m+2}\left(\mathrm{i}\underline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}{}^{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{2}\underline{\mathsf{h}}^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\mathrm{d}\underline{\mathsf{h}}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}-\underline{\mathsf{P}}\underline{\theta}^{0}\right)\,.

We may view
\lambda
as a Weyl connection on the fiber bundle
\mathcal{M}
. Then

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=4\,\varpi^{*}\underline{\theta}{}^{0}\,\lambda+2\,\varpi^{*}\left(\underline{\mathsf{h}}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\underline{\theta}{}^{\alpha}\,\overline{\underline{\theta}}{}^{\bar{\beta}}\right)

is a metric in the Fefferman conformal class
\mathbf{c}
. One can check that any change of contact forms induces a change of metrics in
\mathbf{c}
as described in Proposition
5.8
.

Note that in this particular case, the fibers of
\mathcal{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varpi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underline{\mathcal{M}}
are generated by a null conformal Killing field
k
that is Killing for any metric
g
in
\accentset{n.e.}{\mathbf{c}}
— in this case, we have
\kappa=g(k,\cdot)=2\varpi^{*}\underline{\theta}^{0}
for some corresponding contact
1
-form
\underline{\theta}^{0}
.

A generalisation of this construction to the partially integrable case is given in
[
50
]
, and characterised in
[
100
]
. An appropriate modification of this construction yields Taub-NUT metrics as shown in
[
4
]
and
[
106
]
.

We end this section with the following proposition regarding the most degenerate conformally invariant condition on the intrinsic torsion. Its proof can easily be obtained from Theorem
3.18
, and the geometric interpretations are a direct consequence of the various results of Section
4
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{c},N,K)
be an almost Robinson manifold with congruence of null curves
\mathcal{K}
. Let
g
be any metric in
\mathbf{c}
, and suppose its intrinsic torsion is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{0,0}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\breve{\gamma}_{i}=\breve{\tau}_{\alpha\beta}=\breve{\tau}^{\circ}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}=\breve{\sigma}_{ij}=\breve{\zeta}_{\alpha\beta}=\breve{G}^{\tiny{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt$}}}}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\breve{G}^{\tiny{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt\hbox to5.46666pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.37334pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0933pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt$}}}}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\breve{G}^{\circ}_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}=2(m-1)\mathrm{i}\breve{E}_{\gamma}-\breve{G}_{\gamma}=\breve{B}_{\beta\gamma}=0\,.

Then
\mathcal{K}
is a non-shearing congruence of null geodesics,
(N,K)
is involutive, and:

•

If
\mathcal{K}
is non-twisting, i.e.
\breve{\tau}^{\omega}=0
, the leaf space is foliated by Hermitian manifolds of Gray–Hervella class
\mathcal{W}_{4}
, and
(\mathcal{M},g,N,K)
is a Robinson manifold of Kundt type for any metric
g
in
\accentset{n.e.}{\mathbf{c}}
, and of Robinson–Trautman type otherwise.

•

If
\mathcal{K}
is twisting, i.e.
\breve{\tau}^{\omega}\neq 0
, the almost Robinson structure is induced by the twist of
\mathcal{K}
, and descends to a contact CR structure
(\underline{H},\underline{J})
on the leaf space
\underline{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mathcal{K}
. There is a one-to-one correspondence between metrics in
\accentset{n.e.}{\mathbf{c}}
and contact forms of
(\underline{H},\underline{J})
.

5.5.
The Mariot–Robinson theorem

A solution to the vacuum Maxwell equations on a four-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a
2
form
F
that is both closed and co-closed, i.e.
\mathrm{d}F=\mathrm{d}\star F=0
– here
\star
is the Hodge duality operator. Note that these equations are conformally invariant, which justifies the inclusion of this section at this point. Such a solution is said to be
null
or
algebraically special
if
F
satisfies the algebraic constraint
k\makebox[7.0pt]{\rule{6.0pt}{0.3pt}\rule{0.3pt}{5.0pt}}\,F=0
for some null vector field
k
, and
\kappa\wedge F=0
where
\kappa=g(k,\cdot)
.

The
Mariot theorem

[
55
]
states that any solution to the vacuum Maxwell equations gives rise to a non-shearing congruence of null geodesics. The congruence is generated by
k=g^{-1}(\kappa,\cdot)
. The converse, known as the
Robinson theorem

[
87
]
, is also true, provided that we work in the analytic category: one can construct an analytic null solution to the vacuum Maxwell equations from any analytic non-shearing congruence of null geodesics. We can clearly substitute non-shearing congruence of null geodesics by Robinson structure here, and this move allows for generalisation of the theorem to irreducible spinor fields
[
77
]
. In fact, it makes its understanding more transparent as we shall now explain. We note that the assumption of analyticity is crucial for the implication part of the theorem as references
[
98
,
97
]
make clear.

Following
[
19
,
58
]
, we first note that any null
2
-form
F
must be the sum of a self-dual totally null simple complex
2
-form
\nu
and its (anti-self-dual) complex conjugate
\overline{\nu}
. In the language of the present paper,
\nu
is called a complex Robinson
2
-form and annihilates an almost null structure
N
. The condition that
F
be both closed and co-closed is equivalent to
\nu
being closed. But if
\nu
is closed,
N
must be integrable, i.e.
N
is a null structure (or equivalently, a Robinson structure). Conversely, if
N
is an analytic self-dual null structure, it gives rise to a foliation
\mathcal{N}
by two-dimensional totally null complex leaves on the complexification
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\widetilde{g})
of
(\mathcal{M},g)
– see Remark
4.47
. Take any
2
-form
\underline{\nu}
on the two-dimensional local leaf space
\underline{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}
of
\mathcal{N}
. Then
\underline{\nu}
is necessarily closed, and so is its pullback from
\underline{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. A completely parallel argument applies to the complex conjugate of
\underline{\nu}
, and their sum gives rise to an analytic null solution to the vacuum Maxwell equation on restriction to
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

The complex ‘part’ of Mariot–Robinson theorem was later generalised to even dimensions in
[
41
]
and to odd dimensions in
[
103
]
. Its proof hinges on the same reasoning. We work in the analytic category with a complex Riemannian manifold
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\widetilde{g})
of dimension
2m+2
: a totally null (and thus simple)
(m+1)
-form
\nu
defines an almost null structure
N
, and if
\nu
is closed,
N
is integrable. Conversely, any null structure
N
gives rise to a foliation
\mathcal{N}
by
(m+1)
-dimensional totally null complex leaves on
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. The pullback of any form of top degree on the
(m+1)
-dimensional local leaf space of
\mathcal{N}
is a totally null
(m+1)
-form that is necessarily closed, and also co-closed since it is either self-dual or anti-self-dual.

If we now start with an analytic Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
of dimension
2m+2
, we can apply the above result to analytic Robinson structures by extending them to the complexification of
(\mathcal{M},g)
, which eventually leads to a suitable Lorentzian articulation of the Mariot–Robinson theorem.

5.6.
The Kerr theorem

We now describe all local analytic Robinson structures on even-dimensional Minkowski space
\mathbb{M}
. This problem is conformally invariant, and as such, is most elegantly formulated in the language of twistor geometry. The result, now known as the
Kerr theorem
, was initially motivated by the search for Kerr–Schild solutions to the Einstein field equations
[
44
]
, but came to play a seminal rôle in Penrose’s then-nascent twistor theory
[
75
]
.

We first review the story in dimension four, where analytic Robinson structures are identified with analytic non-shearing congruences of null geodesics. The appropriate framework is the so-called
twistor correspondence
(also referred to as the
Klein correspondence
), which we shall presently describe – see
[
75
,
115
,
36
]
for details. We consider a four-dimensional complex vector space
\mathbb{T}
. The Grassmannian of two-planes in
\mathbb{T}
is a smooth four-dimensional complex projective quadric
\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\mathbb{T}\right)\cong\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{5}
, and as such, is naturally equipped with a complex holomorphic conformal structure. There are two disjoint families of two-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathcal{Q}
, elements of which are called
\alpha
-planes
and
\beta
-planes
, according to whether these planes are self-dual or anti-self-dual. The
\alpha
-planes of
\mathcal{Q}
are parametrised by the points of the projective space
\mathbb{PT}\cong\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{3}
, known as the
twistor space
of
\mathcal{Q}
, and the
\beta
-planes are parametrised by the points of
dual twistor space

\mathbb{PT}^{*}
. Twistor space contains an analytic family
\mathcal{F}
of complex lines parametrised by the points of
\mathcal{Q}
. We thus have a geometric correspondence between
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathbb{PT}
. The Kerr theorem in this context extends this correspondence to one between null foliations in
\mathcal{Q}
and hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{PT}
. To be precise, locally, a null structure on
\mathcal{Q}
is simply a foliation by
\alpha
-planes, and it immediately follows that its leaf space can be viewed as a hypersurface in
\mathbb{PT}
intersecting the lines of
\mathcal{F}
transversely. Conversely, any null structure on
\mathcal{Q}
arises in this way.

To consider real Minkowski space, we introduce a Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathbb{T}
of signature
(2,2)
. Under the action of the stabiliser
\mathbf{SU}(2,2)
of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
,
\mathcal{Q}
decomposes into six orbits, one of which we identify as
compactified Minkowski space

\mathbb{M}^{c}:=S^{3}\times S^{1}
. In other words,
\mathcal{Q}
is the complexification of
\mathbb{M}^{c}
. This comes as no surprise considering that
\mathbf{SU}(2,2)
is the double cover of the conformal group
\mathbf{SO}(4,2)
. Similarly,
\mathbb{PT}
admits the orbit decomposition

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{PT}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{PT}_{+}\sqcup\mathbb{PN}\sqcup\mathbb{PT}_{-}\,,

where

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{PT}_{+}&:=\left\{[Z]\in\mathbb{PT}:\langle Z,Z\rangle>0\right\}\,,\\
\mathbb{PN}&:=\left\{[Z]\in\mathbb{PT}:\langle Z,Z\rangle=0\right\}\,,\\
\mathbb{PT}_{-}&:=\left\{[Z]\in\mathbb{PT}:\langle Z,Z\rangle<0\right\}\,.\end{aligned}

Here,
Z
can be viewed as complex coordinates on
\mathbb{T}\cong\mathbf{C}^{4}
. As a real hypersurface in
\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{3}
,
\mathbb{PN}
is the five-dimensional CR hypersphere, i.e.
\mathbb{PN}
has topology
S^{3}\times S^{2}
and is equipped with a contact CR structure of signature
(1,1)
. It also turns out that
\mathbb{PN}
is the space of null lines in
\mathbb{M}^{c}
.

Now consider an analytic Robinson structure
(N,K)
on some subset of
\mathbb{M}^{c}
. Let
\mathcal{K}
be the non-shearing congruence of null geodesics tangent to
K
in
\mathbb{M}^{c}
, and
\mathcal{N}
the complex foliation by (self-dual) totally null
2
-planes tangent to
N
in
\mathcal{Q}
. Denote their respective leaf spaces by
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{N}}
. Then, identifying
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{N}}
as a complex hypersurface in
\mathbb{PT}
, the Kerr theorem asserts that
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{K}}
is a three-dimensional CR submanifold of
\mathbb{PN}
that arises as the intersection of
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathbb{PN}
. Non-analytic Robinson structures on Minkowski space can also be dealt with as a limiting case
[
98
,
97
,
77
]
.

The generalisation of the Kerr theorem to higher dimensions in the complex case was carried out in
[
41
,
107
]
and is analogous. We consider a smooth projective complex quadric
\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{2m+3}
, which we may identify as the space of null lines in
\mathbf{C}^{2m+4}
— see
[
34
,
33
]
. An
\alpha
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}
is now a self-dual linear subspace of
\mathcal{Q}
of dimension
m+1
and a
\beta
-plane its anti-self-dual counterpart. As before, we define the twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
of
\mathcal{Q}
to be the space of all
\alpha
-planes of
\mathcal{Q}
, and the
primed
twistor space
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{Q}
to be the space of all
\beta
-planes of
\mathcal{Q}
. When
m
is odd,
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{PT}^{*}
, while when
m
is even
\mathbb{PT}\cong\mathbb{PT}^{*}
and
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}\cong(\mathbb{PT}^{\prime})^{*}
. From an algebraic viewpoint, it is convenient to realise
\mathbb{PT}
and
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}
as the spaces of pure spinors, up to scale, for the double cover
\mathbf{Spin}(2m+4,\mathbf{C})
of the complex conformal group
\mathbf{SO}(2m+4,\mathbf{C})
.

Twistor space is a complex manifold of dimension
\frac{1}{2}(m+1)(m+2)
and contains an analytic family
\mathcal{F}
of
\frac{1}{2}m(m+1)
complex submanifolds parametrised by the points of
\mathcal{Q}
. In this complex setting, the Kerr theorem states
[
41
,
107
]
that any local analytic null structure
N
on
\mathcal{Q}
locally gives rise to a complex submanifold
\underline{\mathcal{N}}
of dimension
m+1
meeting
\mathcal{F}
transversely, and every null structure arises in this way. In effect, the submanifold
\underline{\mathcal{N}}
is none other than the leaf space of the foliation tangent to
N
.

From this complex description, it is only a small step to obtain the Lorentzian version of the Kerr theorem. Just as in dimension four, under the action of the real form
\mathbf{SO}(2m+2,2)
of
\mathbf{SO}(2m+4,\mathbf{C})
or its spin analogue,
\mathcal{Q}
decomposes into six orbits, which includes compactified Minkowski space
\mathbb{M}^{c}:=S^{2m+1}\times S^{1}
. To deal with
\mathbb{PT}
, we note that the spin representation for the conformal group
\mathbf{SO}(2m+2,2)
is equipped with a Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
of split signature
[
10
]
, which restricts to a Hermitian form on
\mathbb{PT}
. Using the terminology and results of
[
48
]
, we find that
\mathbb{PT}
admits the decomposition (
5.3
) where its orbits are defined just as in (
5.4
). Their interpretation is as follows:

•

\mathbb{PN}
is of real dimension
(m+1)(m+2)-1
, and consists of self-dual
(m+2)
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathcal{Q}
of
real index
2
: these are the pure spinors (up to scale) that are null with respect to the Hermitian inner product on
\mathbb{PT}
;

•

\mathbb{PT}_{+}
and
\mathbb{PT}_{-}
are of real dimension
(m+1)(m+2)
, and consist of self-dual
(m+2)
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathcal{Q}
of
real index
0
: these are the pure spinors (up to scale) that are spacelike, respectively timelike, with respect to the Hermitian inner product on
\mathbb{PT}
.

In particular, by virtue of being a real hypersurface defined by the vanishing of the Hermitian form on
\mathbb{PT}
, the orbit
\mathbb{PN}
is a
CR manifold
, whose Levi form, one can check, has signature
(m,m)
.

At present, let us take
(N,K)
to be an analytic Robinson structure on some subset of
\mathbb{M}^{c}
with congruence of null geodesics
\mathcal{K}
and complex foliation by (self-dual) totally null
(m+1)
-planes
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{Q}
. View the leaf space
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{N}}
of
\mathcal{N}
as a complex submanifold in
\mathbb{PT}
. Then the intersection of
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{N}}
with
\mathbb{PN}
is a
(2m+1)
-dimensional CR submanifold of
\mathbb{PN}
, which is precisely the leaf space
\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{K}}
of
\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 5.12
.

It is crucial to note that for
m>1
,
\mathbb{PN}

cannot
be identified with the
(4m+1)
-dimensional space of null lines
\mathbb{NL}
in
\mathbb{M}^{c}
. In general
\mathbb{NL}
is a homogeneous space equipped with a
Lie contact structure

[
94
]
, and does not admit any distinguished CR structure unless
m=1
.

There are thus two ways of embedding the leaf space of the null geodesic congruence
\mathcal{K}
associated to a Robinson structure: one as a submanifold of
\mathbb{NL}
and the other as a
CR
submanifold of
\mathbb{PN}
. But only the latter can encode the CR structure of the leaf space of
\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 5.13
.

As stated, the Kerr theorem is only concerned with the involutivity of an almost null structure be it in the complex case or in Lorentzian signature. Further degeneracy conditions on the intrinsic torsion of the null structure will in general impact the way the leaf space of a null complex foliation sits in twistor space. These features have only been marginally investigated so far.
7
7

7

In fact, this was already demonstrated in the odd-dimensional analogue of the Kerr theorem in
[
107
]
. In even dimensions, complex case, this was established in unpublished work by Jan Gutt (Private communication with the third author.)

6.
Generalised almost Robinson geometries

6.1.
Generalised Robinson structures

We now present a variant of the notion of almost Robinson structure, which in dimension four corresponds to the notion of optical geometry presented in
[
110
,
112
,
90
,
88
,
91
,
60
,
111
]
, and which was referred to as
generalised optical geometry
in
[
25
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth manifold of dimension
2m+2
. A
generalised almost Robinson structure
consists of a triple
(N,K,\mathbf{o})
, where
N
is a complex
(m+1)
-plane distribution,
K:=N\cap T\mathcal{M}
is a real line distribution on
\mathcal{M}
, and
\mathbf{o}
an equivalence class of Lorentzian metrics such that

(1)

for each
g
in
\mathbf{o}
,
N
is null with respect to the complex linear extension of
g
;

(2)

any two metrics
g
and
\widehat{g}
in
\mathbf{o}
are related by

(6.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2\varphi}\left(g+2\,\kappa\,\alpha\right)\,,

for some smooth function
\varphi
and
1
-form
\alpha
on
\mathcal{M}
, and
\kappa=g(k,\cdot)
for some non-vanishing section
k
of
K
.

We shall say that the generalised almost Robinson structure is

•

restricted
if any of the
1
-forms
\alpha
in (
6.1
) satisfies
\alpha(k)=0
.

•

of Kerr–Schild type
if any of the
1
-forms
\alpha
in (
6.1
) satisfies
\alpha\wedge\kappa=0
.

We shall refer to
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
as a
generalised almost Robinson geometry
. In addition, we shall call
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
a
generalised nearly Robinson geometry
if
[K,N]\subset N
, and a
generalised Robinson geometry
if
[N,N]\subset N
.

We see in particular that a generalised almost Robinson structure determines a generalised optical geometry
(K,\mathbf{o})
in the sense of
[
25
]
. It is straightforward to check that the two conditions above are well-defined. In particular, the property of
K
and
N
being totally null does not depend on the choice of metric in
\mathbf{o}
, and neither does the notion of orthogonal complement
K^{\perp}
of
K
. A generalised almost Robinson geometry
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
also has an associated congruence of null curves
\mathcal{K}
tangent to
K
.

The following lemma is immediate.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
be a generalised almost Robinson geometry. For each metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
,
(N,K)
is an almost Robinson structure on
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

We shall therefore re-employ the terminology used in the previous sections. In particular, any non-vanishing section of
K
will be called an optical vector field, and any
1
-form annihilating
K^{\perp}
an optical
1
-form. The definition of a complex Robinson
(m+1)
-form as a section of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{m+1}\mathrm{Ann}(N)
does not depend on the choice of metric in
\mathbf{o}
, and neither does the notion of Robinson
3
-form. To see this, we take, for specificity, two metrics
g
and
\widehat{g}
in
\mathbf{o}
related via

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{ab}

\displaystyle=g_{ab}+2\kappa_{(a}\alpha_{b)}\,,

for some
1
-form
\alpha_{a}
. We can choose splitting operators for
(N,K)
for each of the metrics:
(\kappa_{a},\delta_{a}^{\alpha},\delta_{a}^{\bar{\alpha}},\lambda_{a})
and
(\ell^{a},\delta^{a}_{\alpha},\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}},k^{a})
for
g_{ab}
, and
(\widehat{\kappa}_{a},\widehat{\delta}_{a}^{\alpha},\widehat{\delta}_{a}^{\bar{\alpha}},\widehat{\lambda}_{a})
and
(\widehat{\ell}^{a},\widehat{\delta}^{a}_{\alpha},\widehat{\delta}^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}},\widehat{k}^{a})
for
\widehat{g}_{ab}
such that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widehat{\kappa}_{a}&=\kappa_{a}\,,&\widehat{\delta}_{a}^{\alpha}&=\delta_{a}^{\alpha}\,,&\widehat{\lambda}_{a}&=\lambda_{a}+\alpha_{a}\,,\\
\widehat{k}^{a}&=\beta k^{a}\,,&\widehat{\delta}^{a}_{\alpha}&=\delta^{a}_{\alpha}-\beta\alpha_{\alpha}k^{a}\,,&\widehat{\ell}^{a}&=\ell^{a}-\beta\alpha_{0}k^{a}\,.\end{aligned}

Here,
\beta=(1+\alpha^{0})^{-1}
. Let
\omega_{ij}
be the bundle Hermitian structure for
(N,K)
. Then its associated Robinson
3
-form
\rho_{abc}=3\kappa_{[a}\omega_{bc]}
where
\omega_{ab}=\omega_{ij}\delta^{i}_{a}\delta^{j}_{b}
remains invariant under the change (
6.2
).

Generalised almost Robinson geometry arises naturally in the context of lifts of almost CR manifolds as described in Section
4.7
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(\underline{\mathcal{M}},\underline{H},\underline{J})
be a
(2m+1)
-dimensional almost CR manifold, and
\mathcal{M}:=\mathbf{R}\times\underline{\mathcal{M}}
be a trivial line bundle over
\underline{\mathcal{M}}
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is naturally equipped with a generalised almost Robinson structure
(N,K,\mathbf{o})
such that for any metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
,
(\mathcal{M},g,N,K)
is a nearly Robinson structure, i.e. the intrinsic torsion of the corresponding almost Robinson structure
(N,K)
on
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a section of
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[-2\mathrm{i}:1]}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,0}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.18
: the equivalence class
\mathbf{o}
of metrics on
\mathcal{M}
related via (
6.1
) is simply the set of all lifts of
(\underline{\mathcal{M}},\underline{H},\underline{J})
to
\mathcal{M}
for a
fixed
choice of a conformal class of Hermitian forms
[h_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}]
. By construction,
(N,K)
is clearly a nearly Robinson structure. The distributions
N
and
K
do not depend on the lift, and by Lemma
6.2
,
(N,K)
is an almost Robinson structure on
(\mathcal{M},g)
.
∎

The key idea of the next theorem is that it allows us to construct families of Lorentzian metrics equipped with almost Robinson structures sharing the same geometric properties. To be precise, choosing a metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
determines an almost Robinson structure on
(\mathcal{M},g)
, and one may ask which subbundles of the bundle of intrinsic torsions
\mathcal{G}
do not depend on the choice of metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
. We shall call those that remain invariant under such a change
\mathbf{o}
-invariant
subbundles.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
be a generalised almost Robinson geometry. Let
g
be a metric in
\mathbf{o}
so that
(N,K)
defines an almost Robinson structure for
(\mathcal{M},g)
with bundle of intrinsic torsions
\mathcal{G}
.

(1)

The following subbundles of
\mathcal{G}
are
\mathbf{o}
-invariant:

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-2}^{0,0}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,0}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,2}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,0}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,1}
,

(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}
,

[x:y]\in\mathbf{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}
,

(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[-2\mathrm{i}:1]}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
.

Any
\mathbf{o}
-invariant subbundle of a generalised almost Robinson geometry that is not restricted must be an intersection of these.

(2)

Assuming that the generalised almost Robinson geometry is restricted, in addition the following subbundles of
\mathcal{G}
are
\mathbf{o}
-invariant:

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}
,

(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}
,

[z:w]\in\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}
.

Any
\mathbf{o}
-invariant subbundle of a restricted generalised almost Robinson geometry that is not of Kerr–Schild type must be an intersection of these and the ones in (1).

(3)

Assuming that the generalised almost Robinson geometry is of Kerr–Schild type, in addition the following subbundles of
\mathcal{G}
are
\mathbf{o}
-invariant:

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
,

(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[-2(m-1)\mathrm{i}:1]}
,

and

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{0,0}
,

when
m>2
,

\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{0,0}
,

when
m=2
.

Any
\mathbf{o}
-invariant subbundles of a generalised almost Robinson geometry of Kerr–Schild type must be an intersection of these and the ones in (1) and (2).

Proof.

Let
g
be a metric in
\mathbf{o}
. Any subbundle of
\mathcal{G}
that is invariant under changes of metrics in
\mathbf{o}
must also be conformally invariant. Thus, it is enough to consider the subbundles given in Theorem
5.4
, and a metric
\widehat{g}
in
\mathbf{o}
related to
g
by

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{ab}

\displaystyle=g_{ab}+2\kappa_{(a}\alpha_{b)}\,,

for some
1
-form
\alpha_{a}
. Denote by
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\nabla
their corresponding Levi-Civita connections. Then for any
1
-form
\xi_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\xi_{b}=\nabla_{a}\xi_{b}-Q_{ab}{}^{c}\xi_{c}\,,

where
Q_{abc}=Q_{ab}{}^{d}g_{dc}
is given explicitly in Appendix
B
. From (
B.8
), (
B.1
), (
B.2
) and (
B.3
), we immediately find that the subbundles
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-2}^{0,0}
,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,0}
,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}
,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,2}
,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,0}
,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,1}
do not depend on the choice of metric in
\mathbf{o}
. The same clearly applies to the subbundles
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}
for any
[x:y]\in\mathbf{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

We now proceed with the remaining subbundles given in Theorem
5.4
. Throughout,
\mathring{T}
will denote the intrinsic torsion of
(\mathcal{M},g,N,K)
.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}
. Then by (
B.9
) and (
B.4
), we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)^{0}{}_{\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\alpha^{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,.

The LHS is zero if and only if either
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
or
\alpha(k)=0
. The former is equivalent to
\mathring{T}
being a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}
.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}
where
[z:w]\in\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then by (
B.9
), (
B.10
) and (
B.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}z(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{[\alpha\beta]}{}^{0}{}_{0}+w(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)^{0}{}_{\alpha\beta 0}

\displaystyle=(z+2w\mathrm{i})\alpha^{0}\tau_{\alpha\beta}\,.

We immediately conclude that
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[-2\mathrm{i}:1]}
is
\mathbf{o}
-invariant. Suppose now
[z:w]\neq[-2\mathrm{i}:1]
. Then, we must have either
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
or
\alpha(k)=0
.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}
. This subbundle is contained in
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[4\mathrm{i}:-1]}
, which we know is
\mathbf{o}
-invariant provided either
\alpha(k)=0
or
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=\zeta_{\alpha\beta}=0
. In addition, by skew-symmetry we find that

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{[\alpha\beta\gamma]0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

which does not yield any further conditions. Hence,
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}
is
\mathbf{o}
-invariant.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}
. This is a subbundle of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,2}
, i.e.
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=0
. It is also contained in
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[2\mathrm{i}:-1]}
. So, for
\mathbf{o}
-invariance, we must have

\diamond

either
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=\zeta_{\alpha\beta}=0
. In this case, by (
B.11
), (
B.5
) and (
B.2
), we find that

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{(\alpha\beta)\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=0\,.

\diamond

or
\alpha(k)=0
, from which we find

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{(\alpha\beta)\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{(\alpha}\tau_{\beta)\gamma}\,,

which tells us that one must impose in addition
\alpha(v)=0
for all
v\in\Gamma(K^{\perp})
for invariance.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
. This is also a subbundle of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,2}
and
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2,0}
, i.e.
\tau^{\circ}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}=\sigma_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}=0
. It is also contained in
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}
, so for
\mathbf{o}
-invariance, we must have

\diamond

either
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
in which case, by (
B.12
), (
B.6
) and (
B.3
),

\displaystyle\left((\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma 0}\right)_{\circ}

\displaystyle=0\,.

If we assume
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
, then no further conditions are necessary.

\diamond

or
\alpha(k)=0
. Then, again, by (
B.12
), (
B.6
) and (
B.3
), we have

\displaystyle\left((\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma 0}\right)_{\circ}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\left(\alpha_{\bar{\alpha}}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\right)_{\circ}\,,

from which we immediately conclude that
\alpha(v)=0
for all
v\in\Gamma(K^{\perp})
for
\mathbf{o}
-invariance.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[-2(m-1)\mathrm{i}:1]}
. This is contained in
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[2(m-1)\mathrm{i}:1]}
, which is
\mathbf{o}
-invariant if
\alpha(k)=0
or
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=\zeta_{\alpha\beta}=0
. In particular, by (
B.15
), (
B.16
), (
B.4
) and (
B.6
), we have

\displaystyle 2h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{0\beta\gamma\bar{\alpha}}+h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=-4(m-1)\mathrm{i}Q_{0\beta}{}^{0}-\mathrm{i}h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}\left(Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}-Q_{\bar{\alpha}\gamma\beta}\right)\,.

Comparing the two terms on the RHS shows that for invariance to hold, one needs
\alpha\wedge\kappa=0
.

•

Suppose
\mathring{T}
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{0,0}
. In dimension greater than six, this is a subbundle of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1,2}
, i.e.
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
. Then, by (
B.14
), (
B.7
), (
B.4
), we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{0\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}Q_{0[\beta\gamma]}\,.

where

\displaystyle Q_{0[\beta\gamma]}

\displaystyle=-Q_{0[\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma]}-\alpha_{[\beta}(\nabla\kappa)_{\gamma]0}+(\nabla\alpha)_{[\beta\gamma]}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{0[\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma]}

\displaystyle=-\beta(\mathrm{d}\alpha)^{0}{}_{[\beta}\alpha_{\gamma]}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\left((\nabla\kappa)_{0[\beta}+(\nabla\kappa)_{[\beta 0}\right)\alpha_{\gamma]}\alpha^{0}

For
\mathbf{o}
-invariance, we need
\alpha(v)=0
for all
v\in K^{\perp}
, i.e.
\alpha\wedge\kappa=0
. Then we are left with

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{0\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}(\mathrm{d}\alpha)_{\beta\gamma}=2\mathrm{i}\alpha^{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,,

since
\alpha_{a}=\alpha_{0}\kappa_{a}
, which we know it zero. Invariance then follows.

In dimension six,
\tau_{\alpha\beta}
is not necessarily zero. Suppose it is not. Since
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{0,0}\subset(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[-2\mathrm{i}:1]}
, we must have
\alpha(k)=0
, and by (
B.14
), (
B.7
), (
B.4
), we find

\displaystyle Q_{0[\beta\gamma]}=-Q_{0[\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma]}-\alpha_{[\beta}(\nabla\kappa)_{\gamma]0}+(\mathrm{d}\alpha)_{[\beta\gamma]}+\alpha_{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,,

and

\displaystyle Q_{0[\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma]}=-(\mathrm{d}\alpha)^{0}{}_{[\beta}\alpha_{\gamma]}

For
\mathbf{o}
-invariance we must also assume
\alpha(v)=0
for all
v\in K^{\perp}
. Then we are left with

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{0\beta\gamma 0}=2\mathrm{i}(\mathrm{d}\alpha)_{\beta\gamma}+2\mathrm{i}\alpha_{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}=4\mathrm{i}\,\alpha_{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,,

where we have made use of the fact that
\alpha_{a}=\alpha_{0}\kappa_{a}
. So for
\mathbf{o}
-invariance, we must have either
\alpha_{a}=0
, which we rule out, or assume in addition
\tau_{\alpha\beta}=0
and so
\zeta_{\alpha\beta}=0
.

∎

Remark 6.5
.

The
\mathbf{o}
-invariance of the subbundles
(\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[-2\mathrm{i}:1]}
and
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}
in Theorem
6.4
comes as no surprise. These correspond to
N
being preserved by the flow of any section of
K
, and the involutivity of
N
respectively, and these geometric properties do not depend on the equivalence class
\mathbf{o}
.

Remark 6.6
.

Theorem
6.4
should be contrasted with the situation regarding generalised optical geometries, where the
\mathbf{o}
-invariants are precisely the conformal invariants, namely the shear and twist, of the optical geometry of some metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
as pointed out in
[
90
,
25
]
.

6.2.
Generalised Robinson geometries as
G
-structures

Following the original definition of a generalised optical structure of
[
110
,
112
,
90
,
88
,
91
,
111
]
, we express an equivalent definition of a generalised almost Robinson geometry in the following terms:

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth oriented
(2m+2)
-dimensional manifold. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is endowed with a generalised Robinson structure
(K,\mathbf{o})
.

(2)

\mathcal{M}
is endowed with a pair of distributions
K^{(1)}
and
K^{(2m+1)}
of rank
1
and
2m+1
respectively such that

(6.3)

\displaystyle K^{(1)}\subset K^{(2m+1)}

and its associated screen bundle
K^{(2m+1)}/K^{(1)}
is equipped with a conformal structure of Riemannian signature together with a compatible bundle complex structure.

Proof.

Recall from
[
25
]
that a generalised optical structure
(K,\mathbf{o})
is equivalent to the existence of a filtration (
6.3
), where
K^{(1)}
is identified with
K
, together with a conformal structure on the screen bundle
K^{(2m+1)}/K^{(1)}
. Since a generalised almost Robinson structure is in particular a generalised optical structure, it suffices to exhibit a compatible bundle complex structure on
K^{(2m+1)}/K^{(1)}
. But this follows directly from Proposition
5.3
and the fact that the screen bundle does not depend on the choice of metric in
\mathbf{o}
.
∎

A generalised almost Robinson structure on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
can therefore be regarded as a
G
-structure where the structure group of the frame bundle of
\mathcal{M}
is reduced from
\mathbf{SL}(2m+2,\mathbf{R})
(or
\mathbf{GL}(2m+2,\mathbf{R})
if we drop the assumption that
\mathcal{M}
is oriented) to the closed Lie subgroup
H
that stabilises the filtration (
6.3
), together with a conformal structure and compatible bundle complex structure on the screen bundle. One can easily check that
H
has dimension
(m+2)^{2}
.

Under the assumption of real-analyticity, the generalised almost Robinson geometry
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
is integrable as a
G
-structure if and only if there exist local coordinates
(u,v,z^{\alpha},\bar{z}^{\bar{\alpha}})
on
\mathcal{M}
, where
u
and
v
are real,
z^{\alpha}
complex, and
\bar{z}^{\bar{\alpha}}=\overline{z^{\alpha}}
such that

(1)

\frac{\partial}{\partial v}
spans
K
,

(2)

\mathrm{d}u
annihilates
K^{\perp}
,

(3)

(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}z^{\alpha})
annihilate
N
, and

(4)

\mathbf{o}
contains the Minkowski metric
g=2\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v+2\,\undertilde{h}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\mathrm{d}z^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{z}^{\bar{\beta}}
, where
\undertilde{h}_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}
is the standard Hermitian metric on
\mathbf{C}^{m}
.

The characterisation of integrable generalised optical structure was dealt with in
[
90
,
25
]
. In the case of generalised almost Robinson geometries, we have the following result.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
be a generalised almost Robinson geometry with congruence of null curves
\mathcal{K}
. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

There exists a torsionfree linear connection
\nabla^{\prime}
compatible with
(N,K)
and
\mathbf{o}
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}_{u}v

\displaystyle\in\Gamma(N)\,,

for any
v\in\Gamma(N)
,
u\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}_{u}g(v,w)

\displaystyle\,\propto\,g(v,w)\,,

for any
g\in\mathbf{o}
,
v,w\in\Gamma(K^{\perp})
,
u\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
.

(2)

For any metric
g
in
\mathbf{o}
,
(\mathcal{M},g,N,K)
is a nearly Robinson manifold whose intrinsic torsion is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
. In particular, it is of Robinson–Trautman or Kundt type.

Further, in the neighbourhood of any point in
\mathcal{M}
, there exists smooth functions
u
and
v
such that

•

\frac{\partial}{\partial v}
spans
K
,

•

\mathrm{d}u
annihilates
K^{\perp}
, and

•

\mathbf{o}
contains the metric
g=2\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+\underline{h}
, where
\underline{h}
is a family of conformally flat Hermitian metrics of Gray–Hervella class
\mathcal{W}_{4}
smoothly parametrised by
u
,

if and only if any of the conditions (
1
) and (
2
) holds together with the condition

(6.6)

\displaystyle\left(\kappa_{[a}W_{bc][de}\kappa_{f]}\right)_{\circ}

\displaystyle=0\,,

for any
1
-form
\kappa
annihilating
K^{\perp}
, where
W_{abcd}
is the Weyl tensor of any metric in
\mathbf{o}
.

Proof.

We show that (
1
) implies (
2
). Let
\nabla^{\prime}
be as given in (
1
). Note that we can reexpress (
6.5
) equivalently as

(6.7)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}_{a}g_{bc}

\displaystyle=\beta_{a}g_{bc}+2\,\gamma_{a(b}\kappa_{c)}\,,

for some tensor fields
\beta_{a}
and
\gamma_{ab}
, and where
\kappa_{a}
anniliates
K^{\perp}
. Now, the difference between
\nabla^{\prime}
and the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
for some
g
in
\mathbf{o}
can be expressed uniquely by

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}_{a}\alpha_{b}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}\alpha_{b}-Q_{ab}{}^{c}\alpha_{c}\,,

for any
1
-form
\alpha_{a}
,

where

\displaystyle Q_{abc}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\beta_{c}g_{ab}+\gamma_{c(a}\kappa_{b)}-\beta_{(a}g_{b)c}-\gamma_{(ab)}\kappa_{c}-\kappa_{(a}\gamma_{b)c}\,.

This follows from the requirement that
\nabla^{\prime}
be also torsion-free, and (
6.5
) holds. Now choosing splitting operators
(\ell^{a},\delta^{a}_{i},k^{a})=(\ell^{a},\delta^{a}_{\alpha},\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}},k^{a})
for
g
, we find

\displaystyle(\nabla\kappa)^{0}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla\kappa)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\beta^{0}h_{ij}\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla\kappa)_{0j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta_{j}+\gamma^{0}{}_{j}-\gamma_{j}{}^{0}\right)\,,

and

\displaystyle(\nabla\rho)^{0}{}_{\alpha\beta 0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla\rho)_{\alpha\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla\rho)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\beta_{[\beta}h_{\gamma]\bar{\alpha}}\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla\rho)_{0\beta\gamma 0}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{[\beta\gamma]}\,,

and it follows immediately (see e.g. equations (
4.8
) and (
4.9
)) that the intrinsic torsion of the almost Robinson structure of
(\mathcal{M},g,N,K)
is a section of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
. We note that this is independent of the choice of metric in
\mathbf{o}
by Theorem
6.4
.

To prove that (
2
) implies (
1
), we note that since
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,2}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{0}^{1,3}
is a
Q
-invariant subbundle of
\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{3,0}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{1}\cap\not{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}^{2}
, we can simply take the linear connection given in Corollary
4.72
.

The final part of the proof follows from Theorem 7.6 of
[
25
]
and Table
7
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Consider now a generalised almost Robinson geometry
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
for which the Minkowski metric
\eta
belongs to
\mathbf{o}
, i.e. any metric in
\mathbf{o}
is conformal to a metric of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\eta+2\kappa\alpha\,,

for some optical
1
-form
\kappa
and
1
-form
\alpha
. This means that
(N,K)
is an almost Robinson structure for Minkowski space
(\mathcal{M},\eta)
. Using the Kerr theorem of Section
5.6
, one can then generate many non-flat Robinson manifolds.

In the particular case where
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
is of Kerr–Schild type, we recover the original Kerr–Schild metric, considered by Kerr and Schild in
[
44
]
in dimension four, and which is an exact first-order perturbation of the Minkowski metric.

Example 6.10

(The Myers–Perry metric)
.

Let us write the Minkowski metric in standard coordinates
(t,x^{\alpha},y^{\alpha},z)_{\alpha=1,\ldots,m}
in dimension
2m+2
,

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{d}t)^{2}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\left((\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha})^{2}+(\mathrm{d}y^{\alpha})^{2}\right)+(\mathrm{d}z)^{2}\,,

and let

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\frac{r(x^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha}+y^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}y^{\alpha})+a_{\alpha}(x^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}y^{\alpha}-y^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha})}{r^{2}+a_{\alpha}^{2}}+\frac{z}{r}\mathrm{d}r\,,

and

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\frac{Mr^{2}}{1-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\frac{a_{\alpha}^{2}\left((x^{\alpha})^{2}+(y^{\alpha})^{2}\right)}{(r^{2}+a_{\alpha}^{2})^{2}}}\frac{1}{\prod_{\alpha=1}^{m}(r^{2}+a_{\alpha}^{2})}\,.

Here, the radial coordinate is defined by

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\frac{(x^{\alpha})^{2}+(y^{\alpha})^{2}}{r^{2}+a_{\alpha}^{2}}+\frac{z^{2}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=1\,.

Then, the Kerr-Myers-Perry metric in Kerr-Schild form is given by
[
61
]

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\eta+f\kappa^{2}\,.

Being a relative of the Kerr-NUT-(A)dS metric – see Example
4.50
– the Myers–Perry metric admits two sets of
2^{m-1}
Robinson structures corresponding to two optical structures.

As shown in
[
59
]
, these Robinson structures are defined by the eigenspinors of a so-called
conformal Killiang-Yano
2
-form

\xi_{ab}
that is also closed, i.e.
\xi_{ab}
satisfies the overdetermined system of linear first order partial differential equations:

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}\xi_{bc}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{2m+1}g_{a[b}\nabla^{d}\xi_{c]d}\,.

Since these Robinson structures exist for
\eta
, by the Kerr theorem, they must arise from a complex submanifold of dimension
m+1
of twistor space – see Section
5.6
. In
[
107
]
, the construction of this submanifold is given as the locus of a system of polynomials of degree two whose coefficients are determined by the prolongation of the conformal Killing-Yano equation.

Example 6.11

(Fefferman–Einstein metrics and Taub–NUT–(A)dS metrics)
.

Reference
[
106
]
shows that the Fefferman–Einstein metric and the Taub–NUT–(A)dS metric belong to the same generalised almost Robinson geometry.

Example 6.12

(Metrics of supergravity)
.

The metric (
4.41
) together with its Robinson
3
-form (
4.42
) in Example
4.64
belongs to the equivalence class of metrics of an integrable generalised almost Robinson geometry of Kerr–Schild type, and to which the Minkowski metric also belongs.

7.
Generalisation to other metric signatures and odd dimensions

The setting of the present article can be easily adapted to any pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
of signature
(p+1,q+1)
for any even integer
p,q
with
p+q=2m
, where there is also a notion of almost null structure
N
, defined to be a totally null complex distribution of rank
m
. In this general case, the real index
r
of
N
can take any of the values
[
48
]

\displaystyle r\equiv\min(p+1,q+1)\pmod{2}\,.

When
pq\neq 0
, it is therefore necessary to define an almost Robinson structure as an almost null structure
of real index one
. Equivalently, these can be characterised as an optical geometry whose screen bundle is endowed with a bundle complex structure compatible with the screen bundle metric. One has to be cautious in the definition of a twist-induced almost Robinson structure since the screen bundle metric is no longer positive-definite. This difference is also reflected in the pure spinor approach, which now may be of different real indices: beside the purity condition (
3.14
), pure spinors of real index one now satisfy further algebraic conditions
[
13
,
48
]
. Other than these considerations, the properties of the intrinsic torsion given in the present article will apply to different metric signatures.

Finally, one can also define an almost Robinson structure
(N,K)
on a
(2m+3)
-dimensional smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
(or its conformal analogue). Here,
N
is a totally null complex
(m+1)
-plane distribution, i.e. an almost null structure, of real index one so that
K
is the rank-one null distribution arising from the real span of
N\cap\overline{N}
. In dimension three, they are equivalent to optical geometries. Unlike in even dimensions, the real index has to be specified here, since generically, in odd dimensions, the real index is zero
[
47
]
. Another crucial difference is that
N
is now strictly contained in its rank-
(m+2)
orthogonal complement
N^{\perp}
, which makes the algebraic classification of its intrinsic torsion significantly more involved. For instance, one may require the integrability of either
N
or
N^{\perp}
, or both. Nevertheless, these are also relevant to the study of solutions to Einstein field equations in higher dimensions as was shown in
[
59
,
101
]
. The geometry of almost null structures in odd dimensions is investigated in
[
104
,
107
,
103
,
68
]
. Almost Robinson structures can also be defined in signatures
(2p+1,2q+2)
for any
p,q
with
pq\neq 0
as an almost null structure of real index one.

Appendix A
Projections

In this appendix, we define the projections from the modules of intrinsic torsion
\mathbb{G}
to its irreducible
Q_{0}
-modules as given in Theorem
3.15
. We choose a Robinson
3
-form
\rho_{abc}
associated to an optical
1
-form
\kappa_{a}
, and splitting operators
(\ell^{a},\delta^{a}_{i},k^{a})=(\ell^{a},\delta^{a}_{\alpha},\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}},k^{a})
,
(\delta^{i}_{\alpha},\delta^{i}_{\bar{\alpha}})
, with
\kappa_{a}=g_{ab}k^{b}
. We shall be using these to convert index types, with the additional convention that if
\alpha_{a}
is a
1
-form, we shall write
k^{a}\alpha_{a}=\alpha^{0}
and
\ell^{a}\alpha_{a}=\alpha_{0}
.

As usual, the screen space symmetric bilinear form, the Hermitian form and the complex structure will be denoted
h_{ij}
,
\omega_{ij}
and
J_{i}{}^{j}
respectively. Now let
\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}
of
{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
and
\omega_{ab}=\omega_{ij}\delta^{i}_{a}\delta^{j}_{b}
. so that
\rho_{abc}=3\kappa_{[a}\omega_{bc]}
. We streamline notation by setting

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{ab}

\displaystyle:=-\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\kappa_{c}\,,

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{abc}

\displaystyle:=2\,\Gamma_{a[b}\,{}^{d}\omega_{c]d}\,,

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{abcd}

\displaystyle:=-3\,\Gamma_{a[b}{}^{e}\rho_{cd]e}\,.

In particular,

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{abcd}=3(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{a[bc}\kappa_{d]}+3(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{a[b}\omega_{cd]}\,.

Note also that
(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{ab}k^{b}=0
and
(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{abc}k^{c}=-(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{ac}J_{b}\,{}^{c}
. Then it is easy to check

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{a}{}^{0}{}_{jk}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{a0jk}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ajk}+(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{a0}\omega_{jk}\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{aijk}

\displaystyle=3(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{a[i}\omega_{jk]}\,,

\displaystyle(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{a}{}^{0}{}_{0k}

\displaystyle=-(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{aj}J_{k}{}^{j}\,.

Let us first recall the projections from the module of intrinsic torsions to its irreducible
P_{0}
-modules from
[
25
]
:

(A.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Pi_{-2}^{0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-2}^{0}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{-2}^{0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)^{0}{}_{i}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{-1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{ij}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{0}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{0}(\Gamma):=\Pi_{-1}(\Gamma)_{ij}h^{ij}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{1}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\Pi_{-1}(\Gamma)_{[ij]}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{2}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{2}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\Pi_{-1}(\Gamma)_{(ij)_{\circ}}\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{0}\,,&&\Gamma_{ab}{}^{c}\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=(\Gamma\cdot\kappa)_{0i}\,.\end{aligned}

With reference to equations (
A.1
), we also define alternatives to
\Pi_{-1}
and
\Pi_{0}
:

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}:\Gamma\mapsto{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{ij}{}^{0}{}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}:\Gamma\mapsto{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{0ijk}h^{jk}\,.

We note the relation
{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}(\Gamma)_{ij}=-\Pi_{-1}(\Gamma)_{ik}J_{j}{}^{k}
.

In dimension six, there are two further projections from
{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
to the self-dual and anti-self-dual parts of
\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1}
— see
[
25
]
. These will not be needed as they will be subsumed in the projections to
\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,2}
given below.

We shall presently introduce, for each
i,j,k
, a
Q_{0}
-module epimorphism
\Pi_{i}^{j,k}:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k}
with the properties that
{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{q}
lies in the kernel of
\Pi_{i}^{j,k}
, and
\Pi_{i}^{j,k}
descends to a projection from
\mathbb{G}
to
\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k}
. By construction, the kernel of
\Pi_{i}^{j,k}\mod{\mathbb{V}}\otimes\mathfrak{q}
is precisely isomorphic to the complement
(\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k})^{c}
of
\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k}
in
\mathbb{G}
as
Q_{0}
-modules, i.e.

(A.3)

\displaystyle\ker\Pi_{i}^{j,k}/\left({\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{q}\right)

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k})^{c}\,,

i.e.

\displaystyle\left(\ker\Pi_{i}^{j,k}/\left({\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{q}\right)\right)^{c}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k}\,.

Let us define the projections

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{3,0}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{jk}:=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)^{0}{}_{0\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\left(\delta^{\ell_{1}}_{j}\delta^{\ell_{2}}_{k}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell_{1}}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell_{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ijk}:=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{i\ell_{1}\ell_{2}0}\left(\delta^{\ell_{1}}_{j}\delta^{\ell_{2}}_{k}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell_{1}}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell_{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}^{0,0}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{1}^{0,0}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{1}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{jk}:=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{00\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\left(\delta^{\ell_{1}}_{j}\delta^{\ell_{2}}_{k}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell_{1}}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell_{2}}\right)\,.

To guide the reader, we shall note that

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma\cdot\omega)^{0}{}_{\alpha\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ijk}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ijk}

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{jk}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{0jk}\,.

We can define the remaining projections
\Pi_{i}^{j,k}:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{i}^{j,k}
by the properties

(A.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Pi_{-1}^{1,0}(\Gamma)&:=[\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}h^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}]=\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ij}\omega^{ij}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}&:=[\![\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\beta}]\!]\,,&\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ij}&:=[\left(\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\right)_{\circ}]\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}(\Gamma)_{ij}&:=[\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}]\,,&\Pi_{-1}^{2,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}&:=[\![\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\beta}]\!]\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{i}&:=[\![\Pi_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha}]\!]\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{i}&:=[\![\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}h^{\beta\bar{\alpha}}]\!]=\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{jki}h^{jk}\,,&\Pi_{0}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ijk}&:=[\![\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{[\alpha\beta\gamma]}]\!]\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ijk}&:=[\![\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{(\alpha\beta)\gamma}]\!]\,,&\Pi_{0}^{1,3}(\Gamma)_{ijk}&:=[\![\left(\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}\right)_{\circ}]\!]\,.\end{aligned}

where we recall that
[\![\cdot]\!]
and
[\cdot]
denote the real spans of the enclosed quantities.

That these are indeed projections is not too difficult to check. We also define a variant of the maps
\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}
and
\Pi_{0}^{1,0}
by

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,1}\,,\Gamma\mapsto{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=[\![\mathrm{i}{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}(\Gamma)_{[\alpha\beta]}]\!]\,,

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}^{0,0}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{0,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=\frac{1}{2m-2}{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}(\Gamma)_{ijk}\omega^{jk}=-\frac{1}{m-1}[\![\mathrm{i}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{0\alpha\beta\bar{\gamma}}h^{\beta\bar{\gamma}}]\!]\,.

One can indeed verify that these satisfy
\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}={\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}
and
\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{i}={\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{i}
. We are now in the position to introduce the following families of maps: for any
[x:y]\in\mathbf{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
[z:w]\in\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{0\times 1})_{[x:y]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{0,0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{0\times 1})_{[x:y]}(\Gamma):=x\,\Pi_{-1}^{0,0}(\Gamma)+y\,\Pi_{-1}^{1,0}(\Gamma)\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,2}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{2,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}(\Gamma):=[x\,\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}-y\,\mathrm{i}\,\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}]\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,1}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{3,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}(\Gamma):=[\![z\,{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\beta}+w\,{\Pi}_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha\beta}]\!]\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[z:w]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{0,0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[z:w]}(\Gamma):=[\![z\,{\Pi^{\prime}}_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha}+w\,\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{\alpha}]\!]\,.

Finally, we give alternative forms of the maps defined above in terms of algebraic relations with
J_{i}{}^{j}
,
\omega_{ij}
and
h_{ij}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Pi_{-2}^{0,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-2}^{0.0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-2}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=\Pi_{-2}^{0}(\Gamma)_{i}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{0,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{0,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{0,0}(\Gamma):=\Pi_{-1}^{0}(\Gamma)\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{1,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{1,0}(\Gamma):=\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ij}\omega^{ij}\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,1}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{k\ell}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{\ell}_{j}-J_{i}\,{}^{k}J_{j}\,{}^{\ell}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,2}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{k\ell}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{\ell}_{j}+J_{i}\,{}^{k}J_{j}\,{}^{\ell}-\frac{2}{n}\omega_{ij}\omega^{k\ell}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{2,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{k\ell}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{\ell}_{j}+J_{i}\,{}^{k}J_{j}\,{}^{\ell}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{2,1}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{2,1}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{2,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{k\ell}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{\ell}_{j}-J_{i}\,{}^{k}J_{j}\,{}^{\ell}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{3,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{jk}:=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)^{0}{}_{0\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\left(\delta^{\ell_{1}}_{j}\delta^{\ell_{2}}_{k}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell_{1}}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell_{2}}\right)\,,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Pi_{0}^{0,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{0,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{i}:=\Pi_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)_{i}\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{k}:=\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{ijk}h^{ij}\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,1}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,1}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ijk}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell m[i}\left(\delta^{\ell}_{j}\delta^{m}_{k]}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell}J_{k]}\,{}^{m}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,2}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,2}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ijk}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell mk}\left(\delta^{\ell}_{(i}\delta^{m}_{j)}-J_{(i}\,{}^{\ell}J_{j)}\,{}^{m}\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{0}^{1,3}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,3}\,,\\
&\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{0}^{1,3}(\Gamma)_{ijk}:=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{0}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell m[k}\left(\delta^{m}_{j]}\delta^{\ell}_{i}+J_{j]}\,{}^{m}J_{i}\,{}^{\ell}-\frac{2}{m-1}\left(h_{j]i}h^{\ell m}-\omega_{j]i}\omega^{\ell m}\right)\right)\,,\\
\Pi_{1}^{0,0}&:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{1}^{0,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto\Pi_{1}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{jk}:=\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma\cdot\rho)_{00\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\left(\delta^{\ell_{1}}_{j}\delta^{\ell_{2}}_{k}-J_{j}\,{}^{\ell_{1}}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell_{2}}\right)\,.\end{aligned}

Note that for the map
\Pi_{0}^{1,3}
, we use the identity
\omega^{ij}(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ijk}=h^{ij}(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ij\ell}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell}
, or equivalently
h^{ij}(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ijk}=-\omega^{ij}(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ij\ell}J_{k}\,{}^{\ell}
. Other useful identities include

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ki}J_{j}\,{}^{k}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{k[i}J_{j]}\,{}^{k}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ki}J_{j}\,{}^{k}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{-1}^{1}(\Gamma)_{k\ell}\left(J_{(i}\,{}^{k}\delta_{j)}^{\ell}+\frac{1}{n-2}h_{ij}\omega^{k\ell}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}(\Gamma)_{ki}J_{j}\,{}^{k}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{k[i}J_{j]}\,{}^{k}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{-1}^{2,1}(\Gamma)_{ki}J_{j}\,{}^{k}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{-1}^{2}(\Gamma)_{k(i}J_{j)}\,{}^{k}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{j}J_{k}\,{}^{j}

\displaystyle=\omega^{ij}(\Gamma\cdot\omega)_{ijk}\,.

For the remaining modules, we record, for each
[x:y]\in\mathbf{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
[z,w]\in\mathbf{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{0\times 1})_{[x:y]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{0,0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{0\times 1})_{[x:y]}(\Gamma):=x\,\Pi_{-1}^{0,0}(\Gamma)+y\,\Pi_{-1}^{1,0}(\Gamma)\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,2}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{2,0}\,,\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 2})_{[x:y]}(\Gamma):=x\,\Pi_{-1}^{1,2}(\Gamma)_{ik}J_{j}\,{}^{k}+y\,\Pi_{-1}^{2,0}(\Gamma)_{ij}\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{1,1}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{-1}^{3,0}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{-1}^{1\times 3})_{[z:w]}(\Gamma):=\Re\left(z\,{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ij}+w\,{\Pi}_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\Im\left(z\,{\Pi^{\prime}}_{-1}^{1,1}(\Gamma)_{ik}+w\,\Pi_{-1}^{3,0}(\Gamma)_{ik}\right)J_{j}{}^{k}\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[z:w]}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{0}^{0,0}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{0}^{1,0}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma\mapsto(\Pi_{0}^{0\times 1})_{[z:w]}(\Gamma):=\Re\left(z\,\Pi_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{i}+w\,\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\Im\left(z\,\Pi_{0}^{0,0}(\Gamma)_{j}+w\,\Pi_{0}^{1,0}(\Gamma)_{j}\right)J_{i}{}^{j}\,,

where
\Re(\cdot)
and
\Im(\cdot)
denote the real and imaginary parts respectively.

Appendix B
Generalised almost Robinson geometry — connections

Let
(\mathcal{M},N,K,\mathbf{o})
be a generalised almost Robinson geometry, and let
g
and
\widehat{g}
be two metrics in
\mathbf{o}
related via

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{ab}

\displaystyle=g_{ab}+2\kappa_{(a}\alpha_{b)}\,,

for some
1
-form
\alpha_{a}
. Denote by
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\nabla
their corresponding Levi-Civita connections. Then for any
1
-form
\nu_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\nu_{b}=\nabla_{a}\nu_{b}-Q_{ab}{}^{c}\nu_{c}\,,

where
Q_{abc}=Q_{ab}{}^{d}g_{dc}
is given by

Q_{abc}+Q_{ab}{}^{d}\alpha_{c}\kappa_{d}+Q_{ab}{}^{d}\kappa_{c}\alpha_{d}=-(\nabla_{c}\kappa_{(a})\alpha_{b)}-(\nabla_{c}\alpha_{(a})\kappa_{b)}\\
+(\nabla_{(a}\kappa_{b)})\alpha_{c}+(\nabla_{(a}\alpha_{b)})\kappa_{c}+\alpha_{(a}(\nabla_{b)}\kappa_{c})+\kappa_{(a}(\nabla_{b)}\alpha_{c})

Set
\beta=(1+\alpha^{0})^{-1}
. Contracting this expression with instances of
k^{a}
,
\delta^{a}_{\alpha}
,
\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}}
and
\ell^{a}
, and using the definitions (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) yields

(B.1)

\displaystyle Q_{a}{}^{00}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(B.2)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}{}^{0}

\displaystyle=-\beta\gamma_{(\alpha}\alpha_{\beta)}+\beta\alpha^{0}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(B.3)

\displaystyle Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta}{}^{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\beta(\nabla\kappa)^{0}{}_{0}\alpha_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma_{\beta}\alpha_{0}+\alpha^{0}\beta(\mathrm{d}\kappa)_{0\beta}+\beta(\mathrm{d}\alpha)_{\beta}{}^{0}\,.

(B.4)

\displaystyle Q^{0}{}_{\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{(\gamma}\gamma_{\beta)}+\alpha^{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,,

(B.5)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle=-Q_{\alpha\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma}+2\alpha_{(\alpha}\tau_{\beta)\gamma}+\alpha_{\gamma}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(B.6)

\displaystyle Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle=-Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma}-\alpha_{[\beta}(\nabla\kappa)_{\gamma]\bar{\alpha}}+(\nabla\kappa)_{\bar{\alpha}(\beta}\alpha_{\gamma)}+\alpha_{\bar{\alpha}}\tau_{\beta\gamma}\,,

(B.7)

\displaystyle Q_{0\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle=-Q_{0\beta}{}^{0}\alpha_{\gamma}-\alpha_{[\beta}(\nabla\kappa)_{\gamma]0}+(\mathrm{d}\alpha)_{[\beta\gamma]}+(\nabla\kappa)_{0(\beta}\alpha_{\gamma)}+\alpha_{0}\tau_{\beta\gamma}

Now,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\kappa_{b}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}\kappa_{b}-Q_{ab}{}^{c}\kappa_{c}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\rho_{bcd}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}\rho_{bcd}-3\,Q_{a[b}{}^{e}\rho_{cd]e}\,,

so that

(B.8)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\kappa)_{ab}k^{a}\delta^{b}_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\,,

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}_{ab})\delta^{a}_{(i}\delta^{b}_{j)_{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{ij}-Q_{(ij)_{\circ}}{}^{0}\,,

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}_{ab})\delta^{a}_{[i}\delta^{b}_{j]}

\displaystyle=\tau_{ij}\,,

(B.9)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})k^{a}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{\beta\gamma}+2\mathrm{i}Q^{0}{}_{[\beta\gamma]}\,,

(B.10)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})\delta^{a}_{[\alpha}\delta^{b}_{\beta]}k^{c}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\tau_{\alpha\beta}-\mathrm{i}Q^{0}{}_{[\alpha\beta]}\,,

(B.11)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})\delta^{a}_{\alpha}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=G_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+2\mathrm{i}Q_{\alpha[\beta\gamma]}\,,

(B.12)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=G_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}+2\mathrm{i}Q_{\bar{\alpha}[\beta\gamma]}\,,

(B.13)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})\ell^{a}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=B_{\beta\gamma}+2\mathrm{i}Q_{0[\beta\gamma]}\,,

(B.14)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\nabla}\rho)_{abcd})\ell^{a}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\delta^{d}_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}E_{[\beta}h_{\gamma]\bar{\alpha}}-2\mathrm{i}Q_{0[\beta}{}^{0}h_{\gamma]\bar{\alpha}}\,,

(B.15)

\displaystyle h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\rho_{bcd})\ell^{a}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\delta^{d}_{\bar{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=(m-1)\left(\mathrm{i}E_{\beta}-2\mathrm{i}Q_{0\beta}{}^{0}\right)\,,

(B.16)

\displaystyle h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\rho_{bcd})\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}

\displaystyle=-G_{\beta}+\mathrm{i}h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}\left(Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}-Q_{\bar{\alpha}\gamma\beta}\right)\,.

In particular,

-2h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\rho_{bcd})\ell^{a}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\delta^{d}_{\bar{\alpha}}-h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}(\widehat{\nabla}_{a}\rho_{bcd})\delta^{a}_{\bar{\alpha}}\delta^{b}_{\beta}\delta^{c}_{\gamma}\ell^{d}=G_{\beta}-2(m-1)\mathrm{i}E_{\beta}\\
+4(m-1)\mathrm{i}Q_{0\beta}{}^{0}-\mathrm{i}h^{\gamma\bar{\alpha}}\left(Q_{\bar{\alpha}\beta\gamma}-Q_{\bar{\alpha}\gamma\beta}\right)\,.
